Hypersonic Similarity for Steady Compressible Full Euler Flows over Two-Dimensional Lipschitz Wedges Journal: Advances in Mathematics
Gui-Qiang G. Chen, Jie Kuang, Wei Xiang, Yongqian Zhang

Abstract
We establish the optimal convergence rate to the hypersonic similarity law, which is also called the Mach number independence principle, for steady compressible full Euler flows over two-dimensional slender Lipschitz wedges. Mathematically, it can be formulated as the comparison of the entropy solutions in B ​ V ∩ L 1 BV\cap L^{1} between the two initial-boundary value problems for the compressible full Euler equations with parameter τ > 0 \tau>0 and the hypersonic small-disturbance equations (the scaled compressible full Euler equations with parameter τ = 0 \tau=0 ) with curved characteristic boundaries. We establish the L 1 L^{1} –convergence estimate of these two solutions with the optimal convergence rate, which justifies the Van Dyke’s similarity theory rigorously for the compressible full Euler flows. This is the first mathematical result on the comparison of two solutions of the compressible Euler equations with characteristic boundary conditions. To achieve this, we first employ the special structures of the two systems and establish the global existence and the L 1 L^{1} –stability of the entropy solutions via the wave-front tracking scheme under the smallness assumption on the total variation of both the initial data and the tangential slope function of the wedge boundary. Based on the L 1 L^{1} –stability properties of the approximate solutions to the scaled equations with parameter τ > 0 \tau>0 , a uniform Lipschtiz continuous map with respect to the initial data and the wedge boundary is obtained, which is the first time for the characteristic boundary conditions. Next, we compare the solutions given by the Riemann solvers of the two systems by taking the boundary perturbations into account case by case. Then, for a given fixed hypersonic similarity parameter, as the Mach number tends to infinity, by employing the Lipschitz continuous properties of the map, we establish the desired L 1 L^{1} –convergence estimate with the optimal convergence rate. Finally, we show the optimality of the convergence rate by investigating a special solution.



2010 MSC
35B20, 35D30, 35Q31, 35L65, 76J20, 76L05, 76N10

1
Introduction and Main Theorems

We are concerned with the mathematical validation of the hypersonic similarity to
the problem of stationary hypersonic Euler flows
over a two-dimensional Lipschitz wedge (see Fig.
1.1
).
In gas dynamics, we call the supersonic flow is hypersonic when its Mach number is greater than five.
The study of the hypersonic flow can date back to the 1940s due to many important applications in aerodynamics and
engineering (see
e.g.
,
[
21
,
37
]
), which is different from the study of the two-dimensional supersonic
problems (see
e.g.
,

[
7
,
8
,
9
,
12
,
14
,
15
,
23
,
40
,
41
,
42
]
).
It bears important properties of the solution structures,
such as the
hypersonic similarity law
, which is our main focus of this paper.

One of the main reasons for verifying the
hypersonic similarity law
rigorously is to overcome the difficulty
that the density is very small, compared to the flow speed for the hypersonic flow.
Thus, like the fluid behavior near the vacuum, all the characteristics are close to each other,
and the shock layer is very thin.
The validation of the hypersonic similarity law allows us to study a scaled problem in which such a difficulty does not occur
(see
[
2
]
for more details).
In general, we say that two or more different flows satisfy a similarity law if the solution structures
have the similarity with respect to certain nondimensional parameters.

As a paradigm,
the hypersonic similarity can be expressed as follows:
Let
\theta
be the half opening angle of the wedge, and let
M_{\infty}
be the Mach number of the uniform
upstream flow (see Fig.
1.1
).
Define the nondimensional similarity parameter
as

K=\theta M_{\infty}

(1.1)

(see also (127.3) in Landau-Lifschitz
[
31
, page 482]
for more details).
Then the hypersonic similarity is that, for a fixed similarity parameter
K
,
the flow structures are similar under scaling if the Mach number
M_{\infty}
is sufficiently large.
Actually, after scaling, the flows with the same similarity parameter
K
are governed approximately by the same equations,
which are called the hypersonic small-disturbance equations (see Tsien
[
37
]
and Van Dyke
[
21
]
).
Recently, Tsien’s hypersonic similarity theory was rigorously justified for the two-dimensional potential flow past over a straight wedge
with large data in
[
28
]
and the optimal convergence rate was obtained in
[
11
]
, as well as
over a Lipschitz curved wedge with small data in
[
29
]
.
The main purpose of this paper is to verify the Van Dyke’s similarity law
[
21
]
for the two-dimensional steady compressible full Euler equations
for ideal polytropic gas with the optimal convergence rate.

x
y
\mathcal{S}
\mathcal{S}
U_{\infty}
M_{\infty}>5
\theta
O
K=M_{\infty}\theta

Fig. 1.1:
Hypersonic similarity for the Euler flows over a two-dimensional slender wedge

We remark that a related problem on the hypersonic limit as the Mach number
M_{\infty}
of
the upstream flow tends to infinity
with a fixed wedge was studied successfully in
[
25
,
34
,
36
]
.
That problem is different from the problem under our consideration in this paper
because, for a fixed wedge, the similarity parameter is no longer a fixed number.
In fact,
K\rightarrow\infty
as
M_{\infty}\rightarrow\infty
.
For that problem, the limit solution as
M_{\infty}\rightarrow\infty
was constructed,
which is a Radon measure-valued solution over a straight wedge or a ramp,
and the Newton theory or the New-Busemann pressure law of the hypersonic flow was
justified in
[
25
,
34
,
36
]
.
See also
[
26
,
35
]
for further results for the three-dimensional case.
We remark that there are also some related analyses on the steady supersonic flow past a curved cone with its surface being smooth
or Lipschitz continuous when the upstream flow is sufficiently
large; see
[
10
,
16
,
22
,
32
,
33
,
39
]
and the references cited therein.

The steady compressible full Euler equations for the ideal polytropic gas in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
take the form:

\begin{cases}\mathbf{div}(\rho\mathbf{u})=0,\\[2.0pt]
\mathbf{div}(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u}+p\mathbb{I})=0,\\[2.0pt]
\mathbf{div}\big(\rho\mathbf{u}(E+\frac{p}{\rho})\big)=0,\end{cases}

(1.2)

where
\mathbf{div}
is the divergence operator with respect to
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\mathbb{I}
is the identity matrix,
\mathbf{u}=(u,v)
is the velocity with the horizontal and vertical velocity components
u
and
v
respectively,

p
and
\rho
represent the pressure and the density respectively, and
E
is the total energy given by

\displaystyle E=\frac{|\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{2}+e(\rho,p)\qquad\,\,\mbox{with $|\mathbf{u}|=\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}}$}.

(1.3)

The internal energy
e
,
temperature
T
, and
entropy
S
are given by

\displaystyle T=\frac{p}{\textsc{R}\rho},\quad e=\frac{p}{(\gamma-1)\rho},\quad p=\textsc{A}(S)\rho^{\gamma}\qquad\,\,\,\,\mbox{for $\gamma>1$},

(1.4)

where
\textsc{R}>0
is a constant and
\textsc{A}(S)
is a positive function of
S
. See also
[
7
,
18
,
19
]
.

Let
c=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma p}{\rho}}
be the local sonic speed of the flow. Then the Mach number of the flow is
defined by

\displaystyle M=\frac{|\mathbf{u}|}{c}.

(1.5)

We now introduce the hypersonic similarity law and derive the corresponding hypersonic small-disturbance equations
corresponding to the full Euler equations (
1.2
) mathematically.
Let
\tau>0
be a constant. For a given two-dimensional symmetric Lipschtiz slender wedge with surface
y=\tau g(x)
,
without loss of generality, we consider only the lower half-space domain,
i.e.
,
in the region that
x\geq 0
and
y\leq\tau g(x)

with
g(x)<0
as shown in Fig.
1.1
.
The flow satisfies the following impermeable slip boundary condition along the surface of the wedge:

\displaystyle(u,v)\cdot(\tau g^{\prime}(x),-1)=0.

(1.6)

For a given uniformly hypersonic
upstream Euler flow
U_{\infty}=(u_{\infty},0,p_{\infty},\rho_{\infty})^{\top}
,
define

a_{\infty}\doteq\tau M_{\infty}=\tau\frac{u_{\infty}}{c_{\infty}}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $c_{\infty}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma p_{\infty}}{\rho_{\infty}}}$}.

(1.7)

Mathematically, it follows from (
1.1
) that parameter
a_{\infty}
is equivalent to
K
when
M_{\infty}\to\infty
.
Thus,
a_{\infty}
is now called the
hypersonic similarity parameter
due to its naturality in terms of scaling; see
[
2
,
21
]
.
Following
[
2
,
21
]
, we can now define the following scaling:

x=\bar{x},\ \ y=\tau\bar{y},\ \ u=u_{\infty}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}),\ \ v=u_{\infty}\tau\bar{v},\ \ p=\gamma M^{2}_{\infty}p_{\infty}\tau^{2}\bar{p},\ \ \rho=\rho_{\infty}\bar{\rho}.

(1.8)

See
[
2
, §4.4]
for more details.

Substituting (
1.8
) into (
1.2
) by virtue of
(
1.3
)–(
1.4
), we obtain

\begin{cases}\partial_{\bar{x}}\big(\bar{\rho}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u})\big)+\partial_{\bar{y}}(\bar{\rho}\bar{v})=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\bar{x}}\big(\bar{\rho}\bar{u}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u})+\bar{p}\big)+\partial_{\bar{y}}(\bar{\rho}\bar{u}\bar{v})=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\bar{x}}\big(\bar{\rho}\bar{v}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u})\big)+\partial_{\bar{y}}\big(\bar{\rho}\bar{v}^{2}+\bar{p}\big)=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\bar{x}}\Big(\bar{\rho}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u})\big(\bar{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{v}^{2}+\frac{\gamma\bar{p}}{(\gamma-1)\bar{\rho}}+\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{2}\big)\Big)\\[5.0pt]
\qquad+\partial_{\bar{y}}\Big(\bar{\rho}\bar{v}\big(\bar{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{v}^{2}+\frac{\gamma\bar{p}}{(\gamma-1)\bar{\rho}}+\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{2}\big)\Big)=0.\end{cases}

(1.9)

\bar{x}
\bar{y}
O
\Gamma
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
\mathcal{I}
\bar{\theta}=\arctan g^{\prime}(\bar{x})
\Omega
U_{0}(\bar{y})

Fig. 1.2:
Initial-boundary value problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
)

In the
(\bar{x},\bar{y})
–coordinates, the corresponding fluid domain and its boundary are given by

\Omega=\{(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,\bar{x}>0,\ \bar{y}<g(\bar{x})\},\quad\Gamma=\{(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,\bar{x}>0,\ \bar{y}=g(\bar{x})\},

respectively (see Fig.
1.2
).
Then the boundary condition (
1.7
) now becomes

\displaystyle\big((1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}),\bar{v}\big)\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\qquad\mbox{on $\Gamma$},

(1.10)

where
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{n}(\bar{x},g(\bar{x}))=\frac{(g^{\prime}(\bar{x}),-1)}{\sqrt{1+g^{\prime 2}(\bar{x})}}
is the interior unit normal vector to
\Gamma
.

Finally, the initial condition at
\bar{x}=0
is given by

\displaystyle(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{v},\bar{p})=\big(\bar{\rho}_{0},\bar{u}_{0},\bar{v}_{0},\bar{p}\big)(\bar{y})\qquad\mbox{on $\mathcal{I}\doteq\{\bar{x}=0,\ \bar{y}\leq 0\}$}.

(1.11)

Mathematically, the
hypersonic similarity law
is that, for a fixed similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
,
the structure of solutions of (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) is persistent if
\tau
is small (
i.e.
,
M_{\infty}
is large).
Moreover, it is conjectured that the solutions of (
1.9
)–(
1.11
)
should converge to the solution of the following hypersonic small-disturbance equations
(obtained by neglecting the terms involving
\tau^{2}
) in
\Omega
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{\bar{x}}\bar{\rho}+\partial_{\bar{y}}(\bar{\rho}\bar{v})=0,\\[4.0pt]
\partial_{\bar{x}}(\bar{\rho}\bar{u}+\bar{p})+\partial_{\bar{y}}(\bar{\rho}\bar{u}\bar{v})=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\bar{x}}\big(\bar{\rho}\bar{v}\big)+\partial_{\bar{y}}\big(\bar{\rho}\bar{v}^{2}+\bar{p}\big)=0,\\[4.0pt]
\partial_{\bar{x}}\Big(\bar{\rho}\big(\bar{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{v}^{2}+\frac{\gamma\bar{p}}{(\gamma-1)\bar{\rho}}\big)\Big)+\partial_{\bar{y}}\Big(\bar{\rho}\bar{v}\big(\bar{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{v}^{2}+\frac{\gamma\bar{p}}{(\gamma-1)\bar{\rho}}\big)\Big)=0,\end{cases}

(1.12)

with the initial data (
1.11
) and the boundary condition:

\displaystyle\bar{v}=g^{\prime}(\bar{x})\qquad\,\,\mbox{on $\Gamma$}.

(1.13)

If the
hypersonic similarity law
can be justified rigorously, then the study of the steady hypersonic Euler flow over
a two-dimensional Lipschitz slender wedge can be much simplified, because we do not face the difficulty that the density is small
and the characteristics are so close to each other.
In this paper, we justify this rigorously and establish the optimal convergence rate of the solutions
of problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
)
to the solutions of problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
) in
BV\cap L^{1}
, as
\tau\rightarrow 0
.
This provides more helpful information and insights for the applications.

Denote

U^{(\tau)}\doteq(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)},\bar{u}^{(\tau)},\bar{v}^{(\tau)},\bar{p}^{(\tau)})

as the solution of (
1.9
)–(
1.11
), and use
U\doteq(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{v},\bar{p})

as the solution corresponding to the case that
\tau=0
in (
1.11
)–(
1.13
).
The definition of the global entropy solutions

U^{(\tau)}=(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)},\bar{u}^{(\tau)},\bar{v}^{(\tau)},\bar{p}^{(\tau)})^{\top}\in BV\cap L^{1}

of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) is given as follows:

Definition 1.1

(Entropy solutions)
.

A function
U^{(\tau)}\in(BV_{\rm loc}\cap L^{1}_{\rm loc})(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{4})
is called an entropy solution
of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
)
in
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{+}
if

U^{(\tau)}
satisfies the following
:

(i)

For any
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3},\varphi_{4})\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega})
with
\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{3}=0
on
\Gamma
,

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega}\Big(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{(\tau)})\partial_{\bar{x}}\varphi_{1}+\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}\bar{v}^{(\tau)}\partial_{\bar{y}}\varphi_{1}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\mathrm{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{I}}\bar{\rho}_{0}(\bar{y})\big(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}_{0}(\bar{y})\big)\varphi_{1}(0,\bar{y})\,\mathrm{d}\bar{y}=0,

(1.14)

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega}\Big(\big(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}\bar{u}^{(\tau)}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{(\tau)})+\bar{p}^{(\tau)}\big)\partial_{\bar{x}}\varphi_{2}+\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}\bar{u}^{(\tau)}\bar{v}^{(\tau)}\partial_{\bar{y}}\varphi_{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\mathrm{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{I}}\big(\bar{\rho}_{0}u_{0}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}_{0})+p_{0}\big)\varphi_{2}(0,\bar{y})\,\mathrm{d}\bar{y}=0,

(1.15)

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega}\Big(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}\bar{v}^{(\tau)}(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{(\tau)})\partial_{\bar{x}}\varphi_{3}+\big(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}(\bar{v}^{(\tau)})^{2}+\bar{p}^{(\tau)}\big)\partial_{\bar{y}}\varphi_{3}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\mathrm{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{I}}\bar{\rho}_{0}(\bar{y})\bar{v}_{0}(\bar{y})\big(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}_{0}(\bar{y})\big)\varphi_{3}(0,\bar{y})\,\mathrm{d}\bar{y}=0,

(1.16)

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega}\Big(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{(\tau)}\big(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{(\tau)}\big)\partial_{\bar{x}}\varphi_{4}+\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}\bar{v}^{(\tau)}\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{(\tau)}\partial_{\bar{y}}\varphi_{4}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\mathrm{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{I}}\bar{\rho}_{0}(\bar{y})\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\bar{y})\big(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}_{0}(\bar{y})\big)\varphi_{4}(0,\bar{y})\,\mathrm{d}\bar{y}=0,

(1.17)

and
U^{(\tau)}
satisfies the boundary condition (
1.10
) in the trace sense, where
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{(\tau)}\doteq\bar{u}^{(\tau)}+\frac{1}{2}(\bar{v}^{(\tau)})^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\frac{\bar{p}^{(\tau)}}{\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}}+\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}(\bar{u}^{(\tau)})^{2}

and
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\doteq\bar{u}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{v}^{2}_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\frac{\bar{p}_{0}}{\bar{\rho}_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{2}_{0}
.

(ii)

For any test function
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega})
with
\psi\geq 0
, the following entropy inequality holds
:

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega}\Big((\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)})^{1-\gamma}\bar{p}^{(\tau)}\big(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}^{(\tau)}\big)\partial_{\bar{x}}\psi+\bar{v}^{(\tau)}(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)})^{1-\gamma}\bar{p}^{(\tau)}\partial_{\bar{y}}\psi\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\mathrm{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{I}}(\bar{\rho}_{0}(\bar{y}))^{1-\gamma}\bar{p}_{0}(\bar{y})\big(1+\tau^{2}\bar{u}_{0}(\bar{y})\big)\psi(0,\bar{y})\,\mathrm{d}\bar{y}\leq 0.

(1.18)

We first consider the following problem in this paper.

Problem I.

For a given hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
and a sufficiently small fixed parameter
\tau>0
,
seek a global entropy solution
U^{(\tau)}=(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)},\bar{u}^{(\tau)},\bar{v}^{(\tau)},\bar{p}^{(\tau)})^{\top}\in BV\cap L^{1}

of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) in the sense of
Definition
1.1
,
and then
show the entropy solution
U^{(\tau)}
is
L^{1}
–stable with respect to the initial data
U_{0}
and the boundary data
g
.

Denote

\displaystyle\underline{U}\doteq(1,0,0,\frac{1}{\gamma a^{2}_{\infty}})^{\top},\qquad\underline{\Omega}\doteq\{(\bar{x},\bar{y})\,:\,\bar{x}>0,{\bar{y}<0}\}.

(1.19)

It is direct to see that, if
g\equiv 0
and

U_{0}(\bar{y})=(1,0,0,\frac{1}{\gamma a^{2}_{\infty}})^{\top}
,
then the constant state
\underline{U}
is an entropy solution of Problem I.
We call
\underline{U}
as a
background solution
of Problem I.

Once Problem I
is solved, a natural question is whether we can establish the optimal convergence rate for the entropy
solution
U^{(\tau)}
as
\tau\rightarrow 0
,
which can be formulated as follows:

Problem II
.

Let
U^{(\tau)}
be the entropy solutions of

Problem I
,
and let
U=(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{v},\bar{p})^{\top}
be the entropy solution of
the initial-boundary value problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
).
Then, for a given hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
, can the optimal convergence rate of
sequence
U^{(\tau)}
to
U
with respect to
\tau^{2}
be established for sufficiently small
\tau>0
?

Before presenting our main results of this paper to answer both Problems I–II,
we first make some basic assumptions.

Assumptions
: Both the initial data
U_{0}(\bar{y})
and the boundary function
g(\bar{x})
satisfy
the following assumptions:

\mathbf{(U_{0})}

U_{0}(\bar{y})=(\bar{\rho}_{0},\bar{u}_{0},\bar{v}_{0},\bar{p}_{0})^{\top}(\bar{y})

satisfies
U_{0}-\underline{U}\in(BV\cap L^{1})(\mathcal{I};\mathbb{R}^{4})
and
\bar{\rho}_{0}>0
.

\mathbf{(g)}

g(\bar{x})\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
and
g^{\prime}(\bar{x})\in(BV\cap L^{1})(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})

such that

g(0)=0,\qquad g^{\prime}(0)\leq 0,\qquad g(\bar{x})<0\ \ \mbox{for $\bar{x}>0$},

(1.20)

where
g^{\prime}(\bar{x})
is the derivative of
g(\bar{x})
at the differentiable point
\bar{x}

on boundary
\Gamma
.

The first main result is the existence of solutions of Problem I.

Theorem 1.1
.

Under assumptions
\mathbf{(U_{0})}
and
\mathbf{(g)}
, for a given fixed hypersonic similarity parameter

a_{\infty}
as defined by (
1.7
), there exist constants
\varepsilon_{0}>0,\tau_{0}>0
, and
C_{1}>0

depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
such that,
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0})
, if

\displaystyle\|U_{0}(\bar{y})-\underline{U}\|_{BV(\mathcal{I})}+|g^{\prime}(0)|+\|g^{\prime}\|_{BV([0,\infty))}\leq\varepsilon,

(1.21)

then there exists a uniformly Lipschtiz constant
L>0
depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})

so that the initial-boundary value problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) admits domains
\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}}

and a unique map
\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{x}_{0})\,:\,\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}_{0}}\longmapsto\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}}\qquad\,\,\,\mbox{for all $\bar{x}\geq\bar{x}_{0}\geq 0$},

(1.22)

satisfying the following properties
:

(i)

For any
\bar{x}\geq 0
and
U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}}
,

\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{x})(U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{y}))=U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{y}),

and, for all
0\leq\bar{x}_{0}\leq\bar{x}^{\prime}\leq\bar{x}
and
U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\bar{y})\in\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{x}_{0})(U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\bar{y}))=\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\circ\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{x}_{0})(U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\bar{y}));\qquad

(1.23)

(ii)

U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{y})=\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},0)(U_{0}(\bar{y}))
is the entropy solution
of
Problem I

for any
U_{0}(\bar{y})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}

and satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\bar{x}>0}\|U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\cdot)-\underline{U}\|_{L^{\infty}({(-\infty,g(\bar{x})))}}

\displaystyle\,\,+\sup_{\bar{x}>0}\|U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\cdot)-\underline{U}\|_{BV((-\infty,g(\bar{x})))}\leq C_{1}\varepsilon,

(1.24)

where constant
C_{1}>0
depends only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
;

(iii)

If
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\tau)}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\bar{x}}
are the map and domains corresponding
to another boundary
\bar{y}=\tilde{g}(\bar{x})
, then, for any

U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},{\bar{y}})\in\mathcal{D}_{\bar{x}_{0}}
,
V^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},{\bar{y}})\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\bar{x}_{0}}
,
and
\bar{x}^{\prime\prime}>\bar{x}^{\prime}\geq\bar{x}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{x}_{0})(U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\cdot))-\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}^{\prime\prime},\bar{x}_{0})(V^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\cdot))\big\|_{L^{1}((-\infty,\hat{g}(\bar{x}^{\prime\prime})))}

\displaystyle\leq L\big(|\bar{x}^{\prime}-\bar{x}^{\prime\prime}|+\|U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\cdot)-V^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\cdot)\|_{L^{1}((-\infty,\hat{g}(\bar{x}^{\prime})))}+\|g^{\prime}-\tilde{g}^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}((\bar{x}_{0},\infty))}\big),

where
\,\hat{g}(\bar{x})=\max\{g(\bar{x}),\tilde{g}(\bar{x})\}
,

\,\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{x}_{0})(U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\cdot))\doteq\underline{U}
on
(g(\bar{x}),\hat{g}(\bar{x}^{\prime}))
,

and
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}^{\prime\prime},\bar{x}_{0})(V^{(\tau)}(\bar{x}_{0},\cdot))\doteq\underline{U}
on
(\tilde{g}(\bar{x}),\hat{g}(\bar{x}^{\prime}))
.

In Theorem
1.1
and in the sequel,
\|\cdot\|_{BV}
represents the total variation of the state on the domain under consideration.

Based on Theorem
1.1
, our second main result is to provide a positive answer to Problem II.

Theorem 1.2
.

Under the same assumptions in
Theorem
1.1
, let

U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\bar{y})

be the entropy solution of
Problem I

as given in
Theorem
1.1
.
Let
U(\bar{x},\bar{y})

be the entropy solution of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
)
given in
Proposition A.2
.
Then there exist small parameters
\varepsilon^{*}_{0}<\varepsilon_{0}
and
\tau^{*}_{0}<\tau_{0}

depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
such that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}^{*})
and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0}^{*})
,
the following optimal
L^{1}
–convergence rate
holds
:

\displaystyle\|U^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},\cdot)-U(\bar{x},\cdot)\|_{L^{1}((-\infty,g(\bar{x})))}\leq C_{2}\bar{x}\tau^{2},

(1.26)

where
C_{2}>0
depends only on
\|U_{0}(\cdot)\|_{BV(\mathcal{I})}
,
|g^{\prime}(0)|
,
\|g^{\prime}(\cdot)\|_{BV(\mathbb{R}_{+})}
, and
L
,
but is independent of
\tau
and
\bar{x}
.

Since
U^{(\tau)}=\big(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)},\bar{u}^{(\tau)},\bar{v}^{(\tau)},\bar{p}^{(\tau)}\big)^{\top}

and
U=\big(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{v},\bar{p}\big)^{\top}
, estimate (
1.26
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)}-\bar{\rho},\bar{u}^{(\tau)}-\bar{u},\bar{v}^{(\tau)}-\bar{v},\bar{p}^{(\tau)}-\bar{p}\big)(\bar{x},\cdot)\big\|_{L^{1}((-\infty,g(\bar{x})))}\leq C_{2}\bar{x}\tau^{2}.

(1.27)

The order of
\tau^{2}
in Theorem
1.2
for a finite length of the wedge is consistent with
what the Newtonian-Busemann law is predicted, which states that the error term should be of order
\tau^{2}

(see
[
2
, (3.29), pp. 67]
for more details).
Moreover, this
L^{1}
–convergence rate in (
1.27
) is optimal for the infinite length of wedge,
as indicated by a special solution to be demonstrated in the last part of §4 below.

Mathematically, the main difficulties for solving Problems I–II are that the wedge boundary conditions are both characteristic.
One of the boundary conditions is of Neumann-type, while the other one is of Dirichlet-type.
Both systems considered have two characteristics with multiplicity, and the corresponding characteristic fields are linearly degenerate.
They are different from the ones studied in
[
17
,
20
,
29
]
for the non-characteristic cases.
Moreover, due to the curved boundary, the error estimate of the semigroup for the Cauchy problem as stated in
[
3
]
can not be applied directly
so that the approaches used in
[
5
,
6
,
13
,
43
]

for the Cauchy problem have to be further developed for the initial-boundary value problem with multiplicity characteristic boundary.

In fact,
the comparison between the solutions of the Cauchy problems governed by two different systems
was considered in
[
5
,
6
,
13
,
43
]
,
provided that one of them is the standard Riemann semigroup and the other is a global entropy solution obtained by the wave-front tracking scheme.
As far as we have known,
the result established in this paper is the first rigorous mathematical result on the comparison of two entropy solutions
of the compressible Euler equations with characteristic boundary conditions.

To overcome these difficulties and establish the main theorems, we first need to construct a new Lipschitz continuous map
by carefully piecing together the Riemann solutions with a piecewise straight and characteristic boundary,
and then to establish the error estimate of this map with this boundary.

More precisely, to establish Theorem
1.1
, we first proceed with the following four main steps:

\mathbf{Step\ 1.}
We approximate the boundary:
y=g(x)
, by a piecewise straight curve
y=g_{h}(x)
with a mesh length
h
,
and then construct a global
(\nu,h)
–approximate solution
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
)
for
\tau\neq 0
with boundary
y=g_{h}(x)
and piecewise constant initial data
U^{\nu}_{0}
via the wave-front tracking scheme.

\mathbf{Step\ 2.}
To derive the
L^{1}
–stability of the approximate solution
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
, we construct a weighted Lyapunov functional
\mathscr{L}^{(\tau)}
for the two approximate solutions
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
and
V^{(\tau)}_{h^{\prime},\nu}
corresponding to the initial-boundary data
(U^{\nu}_{0},g_{h})
and
(V^{\nu}_{0},g_{h^{\prime}}(x))
, respectively; see (
2.5
) below for more details.

\mathbf{Step\ 3.}
We differentiate
\mathscr{L}^{(\tau)}
with respect to
x
at the non-interaction point to prove that
\mathscr{L}^{(\tau)}
decreases along the flow direction
x>0
. The proof is divided into the two cases:

(i)
The point is away from the boundary
. This can be dealt with as done in
[
4
]
.

(ii)
The point is near the boundary
. For this case, since the boundary is a characteristic that corresponds to the linearly degenerate field, the linear combination of
{q^{(\tau)}_{i}},1\leq i\leq 4
, in
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathscr{L}^{(\tau)}}{\mathrm{d}x}
can not be canceled by using the usual weighting methods.
Fortunately, we observe that the flow angle is unchanged across the linearly degenerate characteristic field.
Based on this observation, we can establish a new relation of the strengths between the waves of the genuinely nonlinear fields and the linear degenerate characteristic fields; see Lemma
2.9
in detail.
By this relation, we can see that
functional
\mathscr{L}^{(\tau)}
is almost decreasing.
Moreover, by the invariance property of the flow angle and the pressure when crossing the linearly degenerate characteristic field, we can further
establish the
L^{1}
-comparison estimate for the flow slope function with respect to the different boundary curves.

\mathbf{Step\ 4.}
With these, we can show that
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
converges to
U^{(\tau)}_{h}
uniquely as
\nu\rightarrow\infty
. Hence, we establish the global existence of a Lipschitz continuous map
\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}
with
U^{(\tau)}_{h}=\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\bar{x},0)(U_{0})
and an
L^{1}
–stability estimate
with boundary function
g_{h}(\bar{x})
.
Then it follows from the limit:
\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\bar{x},0)(U_{0})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}(\bar{x},0)(U_{0})
as
h\rightarrow 0

to establish the existence and
L^{1}
–stability of the global entropy solution of Problem I.

Similarly, we can also establish the existence of the approximate solution
U_{h,\nu}
of problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
)
for piecewise constant initial data and piecewise straight boundary; see Appendix A for the details.
Therefore, taking the limits:
\nu\rightarrow\infty
first and
h\rightarrow 0
second as above,
we can also establish the existence of the global entropy solution of problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
).

Next, in order to prove Theorem
1.2
, we can proceed in the following way: First, we compare the solutions of the Riemann problems between (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) and (
1.11
)–(
1.13
) case by case.
Then, applying the Lipschitz continuity of map
\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}
of problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
),
we can obtain the local
L^{1}
–estimates between the approximate solution
U_{h,\nu}(\bar{x}+s,\cdot)
of problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
)
and trajectory
\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\bar{x}+s,\bar{x})(U_{h,\nu}(\bar{x},\cdot))
for
s>0
sufficiently small.
Based on these, to obtain the global
L^{1}
–estimate between the approximate solution
U_{h,\nu}
and
trajectory

\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\bar{x},0)(U^{\nu}_{0}(\cdot))
, we establish the following formula
with an approximate boundary
g_{h}
(see Lemma
4.1
):

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\bar{x},0)(U^{\nu}(0,\cdot))-U_{h,\nu}(\bar{x},\cdot)\big\|_{L^{1}((-\infty,g_{h}(\bar{x})))}

\displaystyle\leq L\int^{\bar{x}}_{0}\lim_{s\rightarrow 0^{+}}\inf\frac{\big\|\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\xi+s,\xi)(U_{h,\nu}(\xi,\cdot))-U_{h,\nu}(\xi+s,\cdot)\big\|_{L^{1}((-\infty,g_{h}(\xi+s)))}}{s}\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

Finally,
we let
\nu\rightarrow\infty
and
h\rightarrow 0
to show estimate (
1.26
), which justifies the Van Dyke’s similarity theory rigorously.

The remaining sections of this paper are organized as follows:
In §2, we consider the entropy solutions
U^{(\tau)}
of Problem I via a modified wave-front tracking algorithm
and then complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
Meanwhile, by a similar argument, we can also establish the existence theorem
for the initial-boundary value
problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
)
by the modified wave-front tracking scheme, which is given in Appendix A.
After that, we compare the Riemann solvers with boundaries between systems (
1.9
) and (
1.12
),
and then obtain the local
L^{1}
–estimates between the approximate solution
U_{h,\nu}(\bar{x}+s,\cdot)

and trajectory
\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\bar{x}+s,\bar{x})(U_{h,\nu}(\bar{x},\cdot))
for
s>0
sufficiently small in §3.
In §4, we establish the global
L^{1}
–estimate between
\mathcal{P}^{(\tau)}_{h}(\bar{x},0)(U^{\nu}_{0})

and the approximate solution
U_{h,\nu}
of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.11
)–(
1.13
)
to show estimate (
1.26
). Finally, we verify that the convergence rate obtained in (
1.26
) is optimal
by considering a special solution.

For notational convenience in the following sections,

C>0
always represents a universal constant that depends only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
,

O(1)
is a universal function so that
|O(1)|\leq C
,
and
(\bar{\rho}^{(\tau)},\bar{u}^{(\tau)},\bar{v}^{(\tau)},\bar{p}^{(\tau)})
,

(\bar{\rho},\bar{u},\bar{v},\bar{p})
, and
(\bar{x},\bar{y})
are denoted as
(\rho^{(\tau)},u^{(\tau)},v^{(\tau)},p^{(\tau)})
,

(\rho,u,v,p)
, and
(x,y)
, correspondingly.

2
Modified Wave-Front Tracking Scheme and Well-Posedness of Problem I

In this section, we establish the global existence of entropy solutions of Problem I
by introducing a modified wave-front tracking algorithm
in
BV\cap L^{1}
.
Constructing a weighted entropy functional carefully involving a characteristic boundary,
we further show the
L^{1}
–stability estimate between the two entropy solutions with respect to the initial and boundary data,
and then we complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

2.1
Elementary wave curves of system (
1.9
)

By direct computation,
the eigenvalues of system (
1.9
) are

\displaystyle\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\big(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}\big)v^{(\tau)}+(-1)^{j}c^{(\tau)}\sqrt{\big(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}\big)^{2}+\tau^{2}\big((v^{(\tau)})^{2}-(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)}}{\big(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}\big)^{2}-\tau^{2}\big(c^{(\tau)}\big)^{2}}

(2.1)

for
j=1,4
, and

\lambda^{(\tau)}_{i}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})=\frac{v^{(\tau)}}{1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $i=2,3$},

(2.2)

with the corresponding right eigenvectors:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})=(\frac{\big(1+\tau^{2}(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j})^{2}\big)\rho^{(\tau)}}{(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-v^{(\tau)}},-\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j},1,\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-v^{(\tau)}\big)\rho^{(\tau)})^{\top}

(2.3)

for
j=1,4
, and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{2}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})=\big(0,1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)},\tau^{2}v^{(\tau)},0\big)^{\top},\quad\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{3}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})=\big(1,0,0,0\big)^{\top}.\quad

(2.4)

For
\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq 4
, we have the following lemma
to know that the
1^{\rm st}
and

4^{\rm th}
characteristic fields are genuinely nonlinear, and the
2^{\textnormal{nd}}
and
3^{\textnormal{rd}}
characteristic fields
are linearly degenerate.

Lemma 2.1
.

For a fixed hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
defined in (
1.7
),
there exist positive constants
\tau_{1}
and
\epsilon_{1}
depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})

such that, for
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{1})
and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{1})
,
if
U^{(\tau)}\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(\underline{U})
, then

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})>0\qquad\mbox{for $j=1,4$},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{i}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{i}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})=0\qquad\mbox{for $i=2,3$}.

(2.6)

Proof.

For
j=1,4
, it is direct to see that
\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}
satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j})^{2}-2(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})v^{(\tau)}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}+(v^{(\tau)})^{2}-(c^{(\tau)})^{2}=0,

(2.7)

where
c^{(\tau)}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma p^{(\tau)}}{\rho^{(\tau)}}}
.
Then, taking the derivative on (
2.7
) with respect to
\rho^{(\tau)}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle 2\Big(\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})v^{(\tau)}\Big)\frac{\partial\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}}{\partial\rho^{(\tau)}}

\displaystyle\,\,+\frac{\big(1+(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j})^{2}\tau^{2}\big)(c^{(\tau)})^{2}}{\rho^{(\tau)}}=0.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}}{\partial\rho^{(\tau)}}=-\frac{\big(1+\tau^{2}(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j})^{2}\big)(c^{(\tau)})^{2}}{2\Big(\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})v^{(\tau)}\Big)\rho^{(\tau)}}.

(2.8)

Similarly, we can also obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\partial\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}}{\partial u^{(\tau)}}=-\frac{\tau^{2}\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-v^{(\tau)}\big)\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}}{\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})v^{(\tau)}},\\[2.0pt]
&\frac{\partial\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}}{\partial v^{(\tau)}}=\frac{\big(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-v^{(\tau)}}{\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})v^{(\tau)}},\\[2.0pt]
&\frac{\partial\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}}{\partial p^{(\tau)}}=\frac{\gamma\big(1+\tau^{2}(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j})^{2}\big)}{2\Big(\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}-(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})v^{(\tau)}\Big)\rho^{(\tau)}}.\end{split}

(2.9)

Thus, by direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\big(1+\tau^{2}(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1})^{2}\big)^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}-(\gamma+2)\big(1+\tau^{2}(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1})^{2}\big)\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}-v^{(\tau)}\big)^{2}}{2\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}-v^{(\tau)}\big)\Big(\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}-(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})v^{(\tau)}\Big)}.

By equations (
2.1
) and (
2.7
), we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{1}=-\frac{(\gamma+1)\big(1+\tau^{2}(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1})^{2}\big)\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}-v^{(\tau)}\big)}{2c^{(\tau)}\sqrt{\big(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}\big)^{2}+\big((v^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)}}.

Similarly, we also have

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{4}=\frac{(\gamma+1)\big(1+\tau^{2}(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4})^{2}\big)\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4}-v^{(\tau)}\big)}{2c^{(\tau)}\sqrt{\big(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}\big)^{2}+\big((v^{(\tau)})^{2}-\tau^{2}(c^{(\tau)})^{2}\big)}}.

Then it implies that, at the background solution
U^{(\tau)}=\underline{U}
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{j}\Big|_{U^{(\tau)}=\underline{U}}=\frac{(\gamma+1)a^{4}_{\infty}}{2(a^{2}_{\infty}-\tau^{2})^{2}}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $j=1,4$}.

Therefore, we can choose small constants
\tau_{1}>0
and
\epsilon_{1}>0
depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})

such that, for
\tau\in(0,\tau_{1})
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{1})
,

\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{j}>0
for
j=1,4
.

In the same way, by direct computation, we see that

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{2}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{2}=\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{3}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{3}=0.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.1
.

If we define

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})={e}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\,\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\qquad\mbox{for $j=1,2,3,4$},

(2.10)

where
{e}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})=\big(\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{r}}^{(\tau)}_{j}\big)^{-1}

for
j=1,4
, and
{e}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})=1
for
j=2,3
,
then

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\cdot\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\equiv 1\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(\underline{U})$ for $j=1,4$},

\displaystyle\nabla_{U^{(\tau)}}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\cdot\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)},\tau^{2})\equiv 0\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(\underline{U})$ for $j=2,3$},

for some
\epsilon>0
and
\tau>0
sufficiently small.
Moreover, at the background state
U^{(\tau)}=\underline{U}
,

\displaystyle{e}^{(\tau)}_{1}(\underline{U},\tau^{2})={e}^{(\tau)}_{4}(\underline{U},\tau^{2})=\frac{2(a^{2}_{\infty}-\tau^{2})^{2}}{(\gamma+1)a^{4}_{\infty}},

(2.11)

from the proof of
Lemma
2.1
.
Then
{e}^{(\tau)}_{1}(\underline{U},\tau^{2})={e}^{(\tau)}_{4}(\underline{U},\tau^{2})>0

for some
\tau\in(0,\tau_{1})
with
\tau_{1}
depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
.

Based on Remark
2.1
, following the ideas in
[
27
,
38
]
,
we can parameterize the elementary wave curves.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let a hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
defined in (
1.7
) be given.
Then, for any given constant state

U^{(\tau)}_{b}\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{1}}(\underline{U})
,
the
j^{\rm th}
physical admissible wave curves in
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{1}}(\underline{U})

can be parameterized by
\alpha_{j}
with

\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}\mapsto\Phi^{(\tau)}_{j}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}};U^{(\tau)}_{b})
, where

\Phi^{(\tau)}_{j}\in C^{2}\big((-\delta_{1},\delta_{1})\times\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{1}}(\underline{U})\times(0,\tau_{1})\big)

satisfies

\displaystyle\left.\Phi^{(\tau)}_{j}\right|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}=0}=U^{(\tau)}_{b},\quad\left.\frac{\partial\Phi^{(\tau)}_{j}}{\partial\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}}\right|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}=0}=\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\qquad\mbox{for $1\leq j\leq 4$},\qquad

(2.12)

for some
\delta_{1}>0
.
Moreover, for
j=1,4
, if
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}>0
,
we call it the rarefaction wave and denote the corresponding elementary wave curve
by
\mathcal{R}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)}_{b})\cap\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{1}}(\underline{U})
;

while, if
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}<0
, we call it the shock wave and denote the corresponding
elementary wave curve by
\mathcal{S}^{(\tau)}_{j}(U^{(\tau)}_{b})\cap\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{1}}(\underline{U})
.

From now on, we denote
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4})
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\big(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{2}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{3}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}\big)
, and

\displaystyle\Phi^{(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\doteq\Phi^{(\tau)}_{4}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}};\Phi^{(\tau)}_{3}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{3}};\Phi^{(\tau)}_{2}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{2}};\Phi^{(\tau)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),\tau^{2}\big),\tau^{2}),\tau^{2}).

2.2
Solvability of the Riemann problems

In this subsection, we study the Riemann problems, including the lateral Riemann problems (with boundaries).

We first consider the Riemann problem for system (
1.9
) without the boundary as

\begin{cases}{\rm System}~\eqref{eq:1.11},\\[4.0pt]
\left.U^{(\tau)}\right|_{x=\hat{x}_{0}}=\begin{cases}U^{(\tau)}_{a}\quad&\mbox{for $y>\hat{y}_{0}$},\\[4.0pt]
U^{(\tau)}_{b}\quad&\mbox{for $y<\hat{y}_{0}$},\end{cases}\end{cases}

(2.14)

where the constant states
U^{(\tau)}_{a}
and
U^{(\tau)}_{b}
denote the
above
state and
below
state with respect to
line
y=\hat{y}_{0}
, respectively; see Fig.
2.1
.

\alpha_{4}
\alpha_{2(3)}
\alpha_{1}
\bullet
x=\hat{x}_{0}
U^{(\tau)}_{b}
U^{(\tau)}_{m_{2(3)}}
U^{(\tau)}_{m_{1}}
U^{(\tau)}_{a}
(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0})

Fig. 2.1:
The interior Riemann problem

Then we have the following lemma on the solvability of the Riemann problem (
2.14
).

Lemma 2.3
.

For a given hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
,
there exist constants
\epsilon_{2}\in(0,\epsilon_{1})
and
\tau_{2}\in(0,\tau_{1})

depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
such that,
for any states
U^{(\tau)}_{a},U^{(\tau)}_{b}\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{2}}(\underline{U})

and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{2})
, the Riemann problem (
2.14
) admits a unique admissible solution consisting of
four elementary waves
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4})
with strengths

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{2}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{3}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}})
.
The constant states
U^{(\tau)}_{m_{j}},0\leq j\leq 4
, in the Riemann solution satisfy

U^{(\tau)}_{m_{0}}\doteq U^{(\tau)}_{b},\quad U^{(\tau)}_{m_{j}}=\Phi^{(\tau)}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha_{j}}};U^{(\tau)}_{m_{j-1}},\tau^{2})\,\,\,\mbox{for $1\leq j\leq 4$},\quad U^{(\tau)}_{m_{4}}\doteq U^{(\tau)}_{a},

(2.15)

and the properties in (
2.12
).
Moreover, there exists a constant
C_{2,1}>0
depending only on
\underline{U}
such that

C^{-1}_{2,1}\sum^{4}_{j=1}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}|\leq\big|U^{(\tau)}_{a}-U^{(\tau)}_{b}\big|\leq C_{2,1}\sum^{4}_{j=1}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{j}}|.

(2.16)

Proof.

To obtain the solvability of the Riemann problem (
2.14
), it suffices to show that

\det\bigg(\frac{\partial\Phi^{(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}}\bigg)\Bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}=0}\neq 0.

(2.17)

Notice that

\displaystyle\det\bigg(\frac{\partial\Phi^{(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}}\bigg)\Bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}=0}

\displaystyle=\det\Big(\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{1}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{2}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{3}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),\mathbf{r}^{(\tau)}_{4}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\Big)

\displaystyle=e^{(\tau)}_{1}e^{(\tau)}_{4}\begin{vmatrix}\frac{(1+\tau^{2}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1})\rho^{(\tau)}}{(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}-v^{(\tau)}}&&\hskip-10.0pt0&&\hskip-9.0pt1&&\hskip-9.0pt\frac{(1+\tau^{2}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4})\rho^{(\tau)}}{(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4}-v^{(\tau)}}\\[6.0pt]
-\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}&&\hskip-10.0pt1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}&&\hskip-9.0pt0&&\hskip-9.0pt-\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4}\\[6.0pt]
1&&\hskip-10.0pt\tau^{2}v^{(\tau)}&&\hskip-9.0pt0&&\hskip-9.0pt1\\[6.0pt]
\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}-v^{(\tau)}\big)\rho^{(\tau)}&&\hskip-10.0pt0&&\hskip-9.0pt0&&\hskip-9.0pt\big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4}-v^{(\tau)}\big)\rho^{(\tau)}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=-e^{(\tau)}_{1}e^{(\tau)}_{4}\big(\lambda^{(\tau)}_{4}-\lambda^{(\tau)}_{1}\big)\Big((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)})\rho^{(\tau)}-\tau^{2}\rho^{(\tau)}v^{(\tau)}\lambda^{(\tau)}_{2}\Big).

Then, by Remark
2.1
, at the background state
U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}
, we have

\displaystyle\det\bigg(\frac{\partial\Phi^{(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}}\bigg)\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}=-\frac{8(a^{2}_{\infty}-\tau^{2})^{\frac{7}{2}}}{(\gamma+1)^{2}a^{8}_{\infty}}<0,

where
\tau\in(0,\tau_{2})
with choice of sufficiently small
\tau_{2}>0
.
Therefore, the solvability of the Riemann problem follows from the implicit function theorem directly.
Finally, by (
2.12
) and (
2.15
), we can obtain estimates (
2.16
).
∎

Next, we consider the Riemann problem with a boundary. As shown in Fig.
2.2
,
let
\textsc{C}_{l}(x_{l},g_{l})
,
l=1,2,3
,
be three points on
\Gamma
with
x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}
and
g_{l}=g(x_{l})
.
Denote

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=\arctan\Big(\frac{g_{2}-g_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\Big),\quad\theta_{2}=\arctan\Big(\frac{g_{3}-g_{2}}{x_{3}-x_{2}}\Big),\quad\omega=\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}.

(2.18)

For
l=1,2
, we define

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Omega_{l}=\{(x,y)\,:\,x_{l}\leq x<x_{l+1},\ y<g_{l}+(x-x_{l})\tan(\theta_{l})\},\\[2.0pt]
&\Gamma_{l}=\{(x,y)\,:\,x_{l}\leq x<x_{l+1},\ y=g_{l}+(x-x_{l})\tan(\theta_{l})\}.\end{split}

(2.19)

Let
\textbf{n}_{l}
be the interior unit normal vector to
\Gamma_{l}
,
that is
,
\textbf{n}_{l}=(\sin\theta_{l},-\cos\theta_{l})
.
Consider the following Riemann problem with a boundary
\Gamma_{l}
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\rm System}\ \eqref{eq:1.11}\quad&\mbox{in $\Omega_{2}$},\\[5.0pt]
U^{(\tau)}=U^{(\tau)}_{b}\quad&\mbox{on $\{x=x_{2}\}\cap\Omega_{2}$},\\[5.0pt]
(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}_{\Gamma_{2}},v^{(\tau)}_{\Gamma_{2}})\cdot\textbf{n}_{2}=0\quad&\mbox{on $\Gamma_{2}$},\end{cases}

(2.20)

where
U^{(\tau)}_{b}
is a constant state near
\underline{U}
and satisfies

\displaystyle(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}_{b},v^{(\tau)}_{b})\cdot\textbf{n}_{1}=0\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{on $\Gamma_{1}$}.

(2.21)

\textsc{C}_{1}
\textsc{C}_{2}
\textsc{C}_{3}

Fig. 2.2:
The Riemann problem near the boundary

We have the following lemma on the solvability of problem (
2.20
).

Lemma 2.4
.

For a given hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
,
there exist small constants
\epsilon_{3}\in(0,\epsilon_{1})
and
\tau_{3}\in(0,\tau_{1})

depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})

such that, if
\tau\in(0,\tau_{3})
,
U^{(\tau)}_{b}\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{3}}(\underline{U})
with (
2.21
), and
|\omega|+|\theta_{1}|<\epsilon_{3}
,
then problem (
2.20
) admits a unique solution
U^{(\tau)}_{\Gamma_{2}}
that
connects
U^{(\tau)}_{b}
by a weak
1^{\rm st}
wave
\alpha_{1}

with strength
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle U^{(\tau)}_{\Gamma_{2}}=\Phi^{(\tau)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};\,U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),

(2.22)

such that

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha}=K^{(\tau)}_{b}\omega,

(2.23)

where the bound of
function

K^{(\tau)}_{b}>0
depends only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
, but is particularly independent of
\tau
.

Proof.

From the boundary condition on
\Gamma_{2}
in (
2.20
), we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{b}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega,\theta_{1},U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=\Big(1+\tau^{2}\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\Big)\sin(\theta_{1}+\omega)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\cos(\theta_{1}+\omega),

where
\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}
and
\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{1}
represent the second and third components of
\Phi^{(\tau)}_{1}
, respectively.

By (
1.19
), we see that
\mathcal{L}(0,0,0,\underline{U},\tau^{2})=0
for any fixed number
\tau>0
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{b}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega,\theta_{1},U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}}\Big|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle=\Big(1+\tau^{2}\frac{\partial\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}}\Big)\sin(\theta_{1}+\omega)\bigg|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega_{k}=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle\quad\,-\frac{\partial\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}}\cos(\theta_{1}+\omega)\bigg|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle=-e^{(\tau)}_{1}(\underline{U}_{b},\tau^{2})<0.

Thus, by the implicit function theorem, we can show that there exist small constants

\epsilon_{3}\in(0,\epsilon_{1})
and
\tau_{3}\in(0,\tau_{1})
such that the Riemann problem (
2.20
)
admits a unique solution
U^{(\tau)}_{\Gamma_{2}}
that consists of the
1^{\rm st}
wave
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}
only.
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{b}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega,\theta_{1},U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle=\Big(1+\tau^{2}\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\Big)\cos(\theta_{1}+\omega)\Big|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\sin(\theta_{1}+\omega)\Big|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle=1.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle K^{(\tau)}_{b}\Big|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}=-\frac{\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{b}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega,\theta_{1},U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\sigma_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{\tau}_{b}=\underline{U}}}{\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{b}(\sigma_{\alpha_{1}},\omega,\theta_{1},U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}}\Big|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{1}}=\omega=\theta_{1}=0,U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}}=\frac{1}{e^{(\tau)}_{1}(\underline{U},\tau^{2})}.

This indicates that, for any
\tau\in(0,\tau_{3})
, the bound of function
K^{(\tau)}_{b}>0
depends only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we consider the reflection of the weak waves on the approximate boundary.

Lemma 2.5
.

For a given hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
with
\tau\in(0,\tau_{3})
,
assume that
U^{(\tau),-}_{\Gamma_{2}}
and
U^{(\tau)}_{b}
are two constant states
in
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{3}}(\underline{U})
, as shown in
Fig.
2.3
.
Assume that there is
k\in\{2,3,4\}
such that

U^{(\tau),-}_{\Gamma_{2}}=\Phi^{(\tau)}_{k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}};U^{\tau}_{b},\tau^{2}),\quad\,((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau),-}_{\Gamma_{2}}),v^{(\tau),-}_{\Gamma_{2}})\cdot\mathbf{n}_{2}=0.

(2.24)

Then there exists a constant state
U^{(\tau),+}_{\Gamma_{2}}\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{3}}(\underline{U})
satisfying

U^{(\tau),+}_{\Gamma_{2}}=\Phi^{(\tau)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),\quad\,((1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau),+}_{\Gamma_{2}}),v^{(\tau),+}_{\Gamma_{2}})\cdot\mathbf{n}_{2}=0

(2.25)

with

\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}}=K^{(\tau)}_{r,k}\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}},

(2.26)

where
K^{(\tau)}_{r,k}
is a
C^{2}
-function of
(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}},\tau^{2},U^{(\tau)}_{b})
and satisfies

K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}=0,\ U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}=1\quad\,\,\mbox{or}\quad\,\,K^{(\tau)}_{r,k}|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}}=0,\ U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}=0\,\,\,\,\mbox{if $k=2,3$}.

(2.27)

Proof.

It follows from (
2.24
)–(
2.25
) that, for a fixed integer
k\in\{2,3,4\}
,

\displaystyle\big(1+\tau^{2}\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\big)\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})

\displaystyle\,-\big(1+\tau^{2}\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\big)\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})=0.

(2.28)

Then the existence proof of

U^{(\tau),+}_{\Gamma_{2}}
is similar to the one in the proof of Lemma
2.4
.

Then, to establish the estimate for
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}}
,
we take the derivative with respect to
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}}
on both sides of (
2.2
) and use Remark
2.1
to obtain that, for and
k=2,3,4
,

\displaystyle K^{(\tau)}_{r,k}\Big|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}}=\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}}=0,\ U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}}}\mathcal{L}_{r,k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}},U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{\partial_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}}}\mathcal{L}_{r,k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}},U^{\tau}_{b},\tau^{2})}\Bigg|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}}=\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}}=0,\ U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}_{b})\mathbf{r}^{(\tau),(2)}_{k}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})-\tau^{2}v^{(\tau)}_{b}\mathbf{r}^{(\tau),(1)}_{k}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}{(1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}_{b})\mathbf{r}^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})-\tau^{2}v^{(\tau)}_{b}\mathbf{r}^{(\tau),(1)}_{1}(U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})}\Bigg|_{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}}=\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}}=0,\ U^{(\tau)}_{b}=\underline{U}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{r}^{(\tau),(2)}_{k}(\underline{U},\tau^{2})}{\mathbf{r}^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}(\underline{U},\tau^{2})}

\displaystyle=\delta_{k4},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{r,k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}},U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})=

\displaystyle\,\big(1+\tau^{2}\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\big)\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})

\displaystyle\,-\big(1+\tau^{2}\Phi^{(\tau),(2)}_{k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{k}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\big)\Phi^{(\tau),(3)}_{1}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{1}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),

and
\delta_{jk}
is the Kronecker symbol. This completes the proof.
∎

2.3
Wave-front tracking algorithm for Problem I

Based on the results obtained in §2.2, we now construct the
(\nu,h)
–approximate
solutions
U^{(\tau)}_{\nu,h}
of Problem I via the wave-front tracking scheme
in the following three steps (see also Fig.
2.4
):

1.
Define mesh-length
h=\Delta x>0
, and denote
\{\textsc{C}_{k}\}^{\infty}_{k=0}
for
\textsc{C}_{k}:=(x_{k},g_{k})=(kh,g(kh))

for the points on boundary
\Gamma
for integers
k\geq 0
.
Denote

\theta_{k}=\arctan(\frac{g_{k+1}-g_{k}}{h}),\,\,\,\omega_{0}=\arctan(\frac{g_{1}-g_{0}}{h}),\,\,\,\omega_{k}=\theta_{k+1}-\theta_{k}\quad\,\,\,\mbox{for $k\geq 1$}.

(2.29)

By (
1.21
), there exists a constant
g^{\prime}_{\infty}
such that

\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}g^{\prime}(x)=g^{\prime}_{\infty}
.
Therefore, for any given
h>0
, we can choose an integer
k_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
satisfying
that
\|g^{\prime}(x)-g^{\prime}_{\infty}\|_{L^{\infty}(\{x>k_{*}h\})}<h
.

Now, for sufficiently small
h>0
, we can construct a piecewise straight line
y=g_{h}(x)
with finite corners
to the approximate boundary
y=g(x)
as

g_{h}(x)=\begin{cases}g_{k}+\tan(\theta_{k})(x-x_{k})\quad&\mbox{for $x\in[x_{k-1},x_{k})$ and $k\in[1,k_{*})\cap\mathbb{N}_{+}$},\\[1.0pt]
g_{k_{*}}+g^{\prime}_{\infty}(x-x_{k_{*}})&\mbox{for $x\in[x_{k_{*}},\infty)$},\end{cases}

(2.30)

so that

\displaystyle\|g^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot)-g^{\prime}(\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq h,\qquad\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}(g^{\prime}_{h}(x)-g^{\prime}(x))=0,

(2.31)

\displaystyle\|g^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot)\|_{BV(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq\|g^{\prime}(\cdot)\|_{BV(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.

Then we define the approximate domain:

\displaystyle\Omega_{h}={\displaystyle\cup^{k_{*}}_{k=1}\Omega_{h,k}}\cup\Omega_{h,k_{*}},

where

\displaystyle\Omega_{h,k}=\{(x,y)\,:\,y<g_{h}(x),x\in[x_{k-1},x_{k})\}\qquad\mbox{for $k\in[1,k_{*})\cap\mathbb{N}_{+}$},

\displaystyle\Omega_{h,k_{*}}=\{(x,y)\,:\,y<g_{h}(x),x\in[x_{k_{*}},\infty)\},

and the corresponding approximate boundary:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{h}=\cup^{k_{*}}_{k=1}\Gamma_{h,k}\cup\Gamma_{h,k_{*}},

(2.32)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Gamma_{h,k}=\{(x,y)\,:\,y=g_{h}(x),x\in[x_{k-1},x_{k})\}\qquad\mbox{for $k\in[0,k_{*})\cap\mathbb{N}_{+}$},\\[4.0pt]
&\Gamma_{h,k_{*}}=\{(x,y)\,:\,y=g_{h}(x),x\in[x_{k_{*}},\infty)\}.\end{split}\quad

(2.33)

\Omega_{h}
\Gamma_{h}
x=x_{k-1}
x=x_{k}

Fig. 2.4:
Wave-front tracking scheme for Problem I

Finally, for a sufficiently large parameter
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we can construct a piecewise constant function
U^{\nu}_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|U^{\nu}_{0}-U_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{I})}<2^{-\nu},\qquad\|U^{\nu}_{0}-\underline{U}\|_{BV(\mathcal{I})}\leq\|U_{0}-\underline{U}\|_{BV(\mathcal{I})}.

(2.34)

2
.
By Lemmas
2.3
–
2.4
, at each discontinuity point of
U^{\nu}_{0}
and the boundary corner
\textrm{C}_{0}
,
the corresponding Riemann problem can be solved, whose solution consists of shocks, vortex sheet/entropy wave,
or rarefaction waves. To control the number of the wave-fronts, two types of Riemann solvers are introduced as in
[
3
]
.
One is called the accurate Riemann solver, denoted by
(ARS)
, and the other is called the simplified Riemann solver, denoted by
(SRS)
.
For
(ARS)
, we further partition the rarefaction waves into several small central rarefaction fans (piecewise constant solution)
with the speed of each fan closing to the characteristic speed and the strength of each fan less than
\nu^{-1}
(see
[
3
, pp. 129–130]
for more details).
For
(SRS)
, all the new waves are lumped into a single non-physical wave with a fixed traveling speed that is larger than all the characteristics speed,
and the strength of the non-physical wave is defined by the Euclidean distance between the states on both sides (see
[
3
, pp. 131–132]
for more details).

Then the piecewise constant approximate solution
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
exists along the
x
–direction up to line
x=x_{*}
,
on which there is an interaction point inside or on the boundary (
i.e.
,
x_{*}=x_{k}
for an integer
k\leq k_{*}
, or there is a reflection on the boundary).
If the interaction occurs on the boundary,
we adopt
(ARS)
.
If the interaction occurs inside, to decide which Riemann solver is used after the interaction,
we introduce a threshold parameter
\varrho>0
to
be determined later.
If the two wave-fronts
\alpha
and
\beta
with strengths
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha}
and
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta}
respectively
satisfy that
|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha}\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta}|>\varrho
,
(ARS)
is used. Otherwise,
(SRS)

is used.
Finally, in each construction, we may change the speed of a front slightly, so that no more than two wave-fronts interact,
no more than one wave hits the boundary at the same non-corner point, and no wave-front hits the boundary corner
\textrm{C}_{k}
.

\alpha_{i}
\beta_{k}
\gamma_{\mathcal{NP}}
\gamma_{4}
\gamma_{2,3}
\gamma_{1}

Fig. 2.5:
Interactions between weak waves

Based on Lemma
2.3
, following the standard procedure as done in
[
3
,
38
]
,
we have the following local interaction estimate between two weak waves.

Lemma 2.6

(Local interaction estimate between two weak waves)
.

For a given hypersonic similarity parameter
a_{\infty}
, there exist constants
\epsilon_{2}>0

and
\tau_{2}>0
sufficiently small, depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
,
such that, if three constant states
U^{(\tau)}_{a},U^{(\tau)}_{m},U^{(\tau)}_{b}\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{2}}(\underline{U})

for
\tau\in(0,\tau_{2})
satisfy

\displaystyle U^{(\tau)}_{a}=\Phi^{(\tau)}_{i}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}};U^{(\tau)}_{m},\tau^{2}),\qquad U^{(\tau)}_{m}=\Phi^{(\tau)}_{k}(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{k}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2}),

\displaystyle U^{(\tau)}_{a}=\Phi^{(\tau)}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}};U^{(\tau)}_{b},\tau^{2})\qquad\mbox{for $\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{1}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{2}}.\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{3}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{4}})$},

then

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{j}}=\delta_{ji}\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}+\delta_{jk}\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{k}}+O(1)Q(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{k}})\qquad\mbox{for $1\leq i,j,k\leq 4$}.

(2.35)

Moreover, if the non-physical wave
\gamma_{\mathcal{NP}}
is constructed after the interaction by
(SRS)
, then

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{NP}}}=O(1)Q(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{k}}),

(2.36)

where
Q(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta})
is given by

Q(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{k}})=\begin{cases}0\ \ \ &\emph{if}\ i>k,\ \emph{or}\ i=k\ \emph{and}\ \min\{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{k}}\}>0,\\[5.0pt]
|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{k}}|\ \ \ &\emph{otherwise},\end{cases}

(2.37)

and the bound of
O(1)
depends only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
.

3
. We denote all the fronts in
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}

by
\mathcal{J}^{(\tau)}(U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu})=\mathcal{S}^{(\tau)}\cup\mathcal{C}^{(\tau)}\cup\mathcal{R}^{(\tau)}\cup\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}
,
where
\mathcal{S}^{(\tau)}
,
\mathcal{C}^{(\tau)}
,
\mathcal{R}^{(\tau)}
, and
\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}
denote the shock wave-fronts,
vortex sheet/entropy wave-fronts, rarefaction fans, and nonphysical waves, respectively.
For each wave-front, a generation order is introduced to count how many interactions needed to produce such a front inductively as follows:

(i) All wave-fronts generated by the corner points and the initial data are of order one.

(ii) A wave-front
\alpha
of order
k_{\alpha}
hits the boundary at a non-corner point
(\hat{x},g_{h}(\hat{x}))
.
Then the order of the new wave from
(\hat{x},g_{h}(\hat{x}))
is also set to be
k_{\alpha}
.

(iii) The
i^{\rm th}
wave-front
\alpha_{i}
of order
k_{\alpha}
and
j^{\rm th}
wave-front
\beta_{j}
of order
k_{\beta}

interact at
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
with
\hat{y}<g_{h}(\hat{x})
.
Here we denote the non-physical wave-front as the
5^{\rm th}
wave-front.
Assume that
\alpha_{i}
lies below
\beta_{j}
.
Then the orders of the new wave-fronts are given as below:

\rm(iii)_{1}\,
If
i\neq j
, then the order of the outgoing
i^{\rm th}
wave-front is
k_{\alpha}
and the order of the outgoing
j^{\rm th}
wave-front is
k_{\beta}
.
The order of the outgoing fronts of every other family is
\max\{k_{\alpha},k_{\beta}\}+1
.

\rm(iii)_{2}\,
If
i=j
, then the order of the outgoing
i^{\rm th}
wave-front is
\min\{k_{\alpha},k_{\beta}\}
.
The order of the outgoing fronts of every other family is
\max\{k_{\alpha},k_{\beta}\}+1
.

With these, we can now construct the approximate solution
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
globally, provided that the uniform bound of the total variation of the approximate solution

U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
can be obtained and the total number of the wave-fronts is finite. These will be achieved
in §2.4 below.

2.4
Uniform bounds and compactness of the approximate solutions

We now introduce the weighted Glimm-type functional and apply the functional to obtain the uniform bound
of
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
and to show that the total number of the fronts is finite for each fixed
(\tau,h,\nu)
.
First, let us introduce the notation of the approaching waves.

Definition 2.1

(Approaching waves)
.

The
i^{\rm th}
wave
\alpha_{i}
of strength
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}
and the
j^{\rm th}
wave
\beta_{j}
of strength
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}
,
which locate at
(x,y_{\alpha_{i}})
and
(x,y_{\beta_{j}})
respectively with
y_{\alpha_{i}}<y_{\beta_{j}}
, are called approaching if one of
the following cases occurs
:

(i)
\alpha\in\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{S}^{(\tau)}\cup\mathcal{C}^{(\tau)}\cup\mathcal{R}^{(\tau)}
;

(ii)
i>j
;

(iii)
i=j
and
\min\{\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}\}<0
.

Denote by
\mathcal{A}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x})
the set of all these approaching waves on
x=\hat{x}
.

Assume that the approximate solution
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
, constructed in §2.3, satisfy the following assumptions up to
x=\hat{x}
:

\mathbf{(P1)}

U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
has been defined for
x<\hat{x}
and satisfies

U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(\hat{x}-,\cdot)\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{*}}(\underline{U})
,
where
\epsilon_{*}=\min\{\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}\}
,
\tau_{*}=\min\{\tau_{2},\tau_{3}\}
, and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{*})
;

\mathbf{(P2)}

For
x<\hat{x}
, the strength
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha}
of each front
\alpha
satisfies

|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha}|\leq C\nu^{-1}
.

Then we define the modified Glimm-type functional for
x>0
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(x)=\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(x)+\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(x),

(2.38)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(x)=\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{1}(x)+\sum^{4}_{i=2}\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{i}\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{i}(x)+\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}}(x)+\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{c}\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{c}(x),\qquad\quad

(2.39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(x)=\sum_{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{j})\in\mathcal{A}^{(\tau)}(x)}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}|,

(2.40)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{i}(x)=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{J}^{(\tau)}}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}|\qquad\mbox{for $1\leq i\leq 4$},

(2.41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}}(x)=\sum_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}\in\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}}|,\qquad\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{c}(x)=\sum_{k>[\frac{x}{h}]}|\omega_{k}|.

(2.42)

These constants
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{c}
,
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}
, and
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{i},i=2,3,4
, are chosen such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{c}>\max\big\{|K^{(\tau)}_{b}|+\frac{1}{2},1\big\},\quad\,\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{i}>\max\big\{|K^{(\tau)}_{r,i}|+\frac{1}{4},1\big\}\,\,\,\mbox{for $i=2,3,4$},\\[2.0pt]
&\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}>4C_{2,1}\max\big\{\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{2},\ \mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{3},\ \mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{4}\big\}+1,\end{split}

where
C_{2,1}
is the bound in (
2.16
) in Lemma
2.3
depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
.

We now show that functional
\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(x)
is decreasing with respect to
x
such that
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
can be defined
and satisfies
assumptions (
\mathbf{P1}
) and (
\mathbf{P2}
) for all
x>0
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{c}
,
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}
, and
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{\ell},2\leq\ell\leq 4
, satisfy (
2.4
).
Under assumptions
\mathbf{(P1)}
and
\mathbf{(P2)}
,
there exists a small parameter
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that, for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{*})
,
if
\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)<\varepsilon
, then

\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq-\frac{1}{8}\mathcal{E}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}),\qquad U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(\hat{x}+,\cdot)\in\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon_{*}}(\underline{U}),

(2.44)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}|&\emph{if$(\alpha_i, \beta_j)\in\mathcal{A}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x})$and$y_{\alpha_i}=y_{\beta_j}<g_h(\hat{x})$},\\[5.0pt]
|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}|&\emph{ifthe$i^{\rm th}$wavehitstheboundary$\Gamma_{h}$atpoint$(\hat{x},g_{h}(\hat{x}))$},\\[5.0pt]
|\omega_{k}|&\emph{if$\hat{x}=x_k$},\end{array}\right.

for
i,j=2,3
or
4
, and
k=[\frac{\hat{x}}{h}]
.

Proof.

We prove estimate (
2.44
) case by case.

Case 1
:
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{j})\in\mathcal{A}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x})
and
y_{\alpha_{i}}=y_{\beta_{j}}<g_{h}(\hat{x})
.
Let the strengths of wave-fronts
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{j}
are
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}
and

\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}
, respectively.
Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\tau)}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=(\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{1}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{2}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{3}},\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\gamma_{4}})

(see Fig.
2.5
). Then, by Lemma
2.6
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq C_{2,0}\max\{\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{2},\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{3},\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{4}\}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}|.

Meanwhile, for
\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(x)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq\big(C_{2,1}\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)-1\big)|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}|,

where constants
C_{2,j}>0,j=0,1
, depend only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
.
If
\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)<\frac{3}{4C_{2,1}}
is chosen, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq-\frac{1}{4}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}|.

Thus, it follows from the estimates of
\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x})
and the choice of

\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{G}(\hat{x}-)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)+\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}\big(\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(C_{2}\max\{\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{2},\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{3},\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{4}\}-\frac{\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}}{4}\Big)|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}|

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{4}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\beta_{j}}|.

Case 2
:
\hat{x}=x_{k}
.
Then, by Lemma
2.4
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{1}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{1}(\hat{x}-)=|K^{(\tau)}_{b}||\omega_{k}|,\quad\,\,\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{c}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{c}(\hat{x}-)=-|\omega_{k}|,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{i}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{i}(\hat{x}-)=0\qquad\mbox{for $2\leq i\leq 4$}.

Then it follows from the choice of
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{c}
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)=-\big(\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{c}-|K^{(\tau)}_{b}|\big)|\omega_{k}|\leq-\frac{1}{2}|\omega_{k}|.

Meanwhile, for
\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq|K^{(\tau)}_{b}|\,\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)|\omega_{k}|.

Thus, if
\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq\frac{1}{4\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{b}|}
is chosen, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\big(1-2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{b}|\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\big)|\omega_{k}|\leq-\frac{1}{4}|\omega_{k}|.

Case 3
:
The
i^{\rm th}
wave hits boundary
\Gamma_{h}
at point
(\hat{x},g_{h}(\hat{x}))
.
Without loss of generality, we only consider the case:
i=4
,
since the other cases can be done analogously.
Let the strength of the
4^{\rm th}
wave
\alpha_{4}
is
\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}
.
From Lemma
2.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{1}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{1}(\hat{x}-)=|K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}||\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|,\qquad\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{4}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{4}(\hat{x}-)=-|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{c}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{c}(\hat{x}-)=0,\qquad\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{\ell}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{\ell}(\hat{x}-)=0\quad\mbox{for $\ell=2,3$}.

Then it follows from the choice of
\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{4}
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)=-\big(\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}_{4}-|K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}|\big)|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|\leq-\frac{1}{4}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|.

Meanwhile,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq\big(|K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}|-1\big)\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|\leq|K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}|\,\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|.

Therefore, if
\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq\frac{1}{2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}}
is chosen,
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)-\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\leq-\frac{1}{4}\big(1-\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}|\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)\big)|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|\leq-\frac{1}{8}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{4}}|.

Thus, for all the cases,
if

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}-)<\min\bigg\{\frac{3}{4C_{2,1}},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{r,2}|},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{r,3}|},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}|},\frac{1}{4\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{b}|}\bigg\},

then

\mathcal{G}(\hat{x}+)<\mathcal{G}(\hat{x}-).

Choose

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}=\min\Big\{\frac{3}{4C_{2,1}},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{r,2}|},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{r,3}|},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{r,4}|},\frac{1}{4\mathcal{K}^{(\tau)}|K^{(\tau)}_{b}|},\frac{\epsilon_{*}}{C_{2,1}}\Big\}.

If
\mathcal{G}(\hat{x}-)<\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
, then
\mathcal{G}(\hat{x}+)<\varepsilon
.
This implies

\displaystyle|U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(\hat{x}+,\cdot)-\underline{U}|<C_{2,1}\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)\leq C_{2,1}\mathcal{G}^{(\tau)}(\hat{x}+)\leq C_{2,1}\varepsilon.

Then we obtain the estimates in (
2.44
).
∎

Using Lemma
2.7
and following the arguments in
[
1
,
3
,
17
]
,
we arrive at the following proposition. Because the argument is standard, we omit the proof.

Proposition 2.1
.

Under assumptions
(\mathbf{U_{0}})
and
(\mathbf{g})
, there exist constants

\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}(\varepsilon_{1})>0
and
\tau_{1}<\tau_{*}
depending only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})

such that, if the initial data
U_{0}(y)
and the boundary function
g(x)
satisfy

\displaystyle\big\|U_{0}(\cdot)-\underline{U}\big\|_{BV(\mathcal{I})}+|g^{\prime}(0)|+\big\|g^{\prime}(\cdot)\|_{BV((0,\infty))}\leq\varepsilon

(2.46)

for some
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
and
\tau\in(0,\tau_{1})
,
then there exists a positive threshold
\varrho=\varrho_{\nu}>0
with
\varrho_{\nu}\rightarrow 0
as
\nu\rightarrow\infty

such that the wave-front tracking algorithm introduced in
§2.3

provides an approximate solution
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(x,y)\in\big(BV_{\rm loc}\cap L^{1}_{\rm loc}\big)(\Omega_{h})
globally for all
x>0
.
Moreover, the following estimates hold
:

(i)
There exist positive constants
C_{2,2}>0
and
C_{2,3}>0
independent of
(h,\nu,\tau)
such that

\sup_{x>0}\|U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(x,\cdot)-\underline{U}\|_{L^{\infty}((-\infty,g_{h}(x)))}+\sup_{x>0}\big\|U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(x,\cdot)-\underline{U}\big\|_{BV((-\infty,g_{h}(x)))}\leq C_{2,2}\varepsilon,

(2.47)

and, for any
x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}>0
,

\big\|U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(x^{\prime},\cdot+g_{h}(x^{\prime}))-U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}(x^{\prime\prime},\cdot+g_{h}(x^{\prime\prime}))\big\|_{L^{1}((-\infty,0))}\leq C_{2,3}|x^{\prime}-x^{\prime\prime}|;\quad

(2.48)

(ii)
The strength of each rarefaction-front is small,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha}|\leq C_{2,4}\nu^{-1}\qquad\mbox{for $\alpha\in\mathcal{R}^{(\tau)}$},

(2.49)

and the total strength of the nonphysical waves is small,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha}|\leq C_{2,5}2^{-\nu},

(2.50)

where constants
C_{2,4}>0
and
C_{2,5}>0
depend only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
.

Next, we further investigate properties of the approximate solutions
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
.
To this end, denote by
y=\mathcal{Y}(x)
as a Lipschitz continuous curve
that is a linear combination of
g_{h}(x)
and
\check{g}_{h}(x)
, and
let
\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma)=\{(x,\mathcal{Y}(x))\,:\,y=\mathcal{Y}(x),x\in\Sigma\}
for an interval
\Sigma
.
Denote
\|f\|_{BV(\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma))}
to be the total variation of function
f
along
y=\mathcal{Y}(x)
on interval
\Sigma
.
Then we have

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose that
U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}
be the approximate solutions
of problem (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) constructed by the wave-front tracking scheme in
§2.4
.
Then, when
\varepsilon>0
and
\tau>0
are sufficiently small,

\Big\|(\frac{v^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}}{1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}},p^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu})\Big\|_{BV(\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma_{x}))}\leq C\Big(\big\|U_{0}\big\|_{BV(\mathcal{I})}+|g^{\prime}(0)|+\big\|g^{\prime}(\cdot)\|_{BV((0,\infty))}\Big)

(2.51)

for
\Sigma_{x}=(0,x)
, where
C>0
depends only on
(\underline{U},a_{\infty})
.

Proof.

Let
\omega^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}\doteq\frac{v^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}}{1+\tau^{2}u^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}}
, and
let
y_{\alpha}(x)
be the location of each front
\alpha\in\mathcal{J}^{(\tau)}(U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu})
.
In order to show (
2.51
), we re-define
the modified Glimm-type functional for
x>0
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{(\tau)}_{\mathcal{Y}}(x)=\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau)}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(\tau)}(x),

where
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(\tau)}(x)=\mathcal{Q}^{(\tau)}(x)
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=a,b}\Big(\sum_{i=1,4}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau),j}_{i}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau),j}_{i}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau),j}_{\mathcal{NP}}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau),j}_{\mathcal{NP}}(x)\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=2,3}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau)}_{l}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{l}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau)}_{c}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{c}(x)+\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{\mathcal{Y}}(x),

with
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{i}(x)
for
i=2,3
,
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{c}(x)=\mathcal{V}^{(\tau)}_{c}(x)
,
and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau),a}_{i}(x)=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{J}^{(\tau)}(U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}),\atop y_{\alpha_{i}}(x)>\mathcal{Y}(x)}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}|,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau),b}_{i}(x)=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{J}^{(\tau)}(U^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu}),\atop y_{\alpha_{i}}(x)<\mathcal{Y}(x)}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{i}}|\qquad\mbox{for $i=1,4$},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau),a}_{\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}}(x)=\sum_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}\in\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)},\atop y_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}}(x)>\mathcal{Y}(x)}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}}|,\quad\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau),b}_{\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)}}(x)=\sum_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}\in\mathcal{NP}^{(\tau)},\atop y_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}}(x)<\mathcal{Y}(x)}|\sigma^{(\tau)}_{\alpha_{\mathcal{NP}}}|,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{(\tau)}_{\mathcal{Y}}(x)=\big\|(\omega^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu},p^{(\tau)}_{h,\nu})\big\|_{BV(\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma_{x}))},

constants
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau),j}_{i}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau),j}_{\mathcal{NP}}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau)}_{l}
for
i=1,4,\ j=a,b,l=2,3
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}^{(\tau)}_{c}

are chosen such that
